</td></tr><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-header" style="background:lavender">Personal details</th></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Born</th><td class="infobox-data"><div style="display:inline" class="nickname">Chaudhary Charan Singh</div><br /><span style="display:none">(<span class="bday">1902-12-23</span>)</span>23 December 1902<br /><a href="/wiki/Hapur_district" title="Hapur district">Noorpur</a>, <a href="/wiki/United_Provinces_of_Agra_and_Oudh" title="United Provinces of Agra and Oudh">United Provinces of Agra and Oudh</a>, <a href="/wiki/British_Raj" title="British Raj">British India</a> <br /> <span style="font-size:85%;">(present-day <a href="/wiki/Uttar_Pradesh" title="Uttar Pradesh">Uttar Pradesh</a>, <a href="/wiki/India" title="India">India</a>)</span></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Died</th><td class="infobox-data">29 May 1987<span style="display:none">(1987-05-29)</span> (aged&#160;84)<br /><a href="/wiki/New_Delhi" title="New Delhi">New Delhi</a>, <a href="/wiki/India" title="India">India</a>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1066479718"></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Monuments</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Raj_Ghat_and_associated_memorials" title="Raj Ghat and associated memorials">Kisan Ghat</a></td></tr><tr style="display:none"><td colspan="2">
</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Political party</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Lokdal" title="Lokdal">Lokdal</a> <small>(own party; 1980–1987)</small></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Other political<br />affiliations</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Indian_National_Congress" title="Indian National Congress">Indian National Congress</a> <small>(Before 1967)</small><br /><a href="/wiki/Bharatiya_Lok_Dal" title="Bharatiya Lok Dal">Bharatiya Lok Dal</a> <small>(own party;1967–1977)</small><br /><a href="/wiki/Janata_Party" title="Janata Party">Janata Party</a> <small>(1977–1979)</small><br /><a href="/wiki/Janata_Party_(Secular)" title="Janata Party (Secular)">Janata Party (Secular)</a> <small>(1979–1980)</small></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Spouse</th><td class="infobox-data"><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1151524712">.mw-parser-output .marriage-line-margin2px{line-height:0;margin-bottom:-2px}.mw-parser-output .marriage-line-margin3px{line-height:0;margin-bottom:-3px}.mw-parser-output .marriage-display-ws{display:inline;white-space:nowrap}</style>
<div class="marriage-display-ws"><div style="display:inline-block;line-height:normal;"><a href="/wiki/Gayatri_Devi_(Uttar_Pradesh_politician)" title="Gayatri Devi (Uttar Pradesh politician)">Gayatri Devi</a></div>&#32;<div style="display:inline-block;">&#8203;</div>&#40;<abbr title="married">m.</abbr>&#160;1925&#41;<wbr />&#8203;</div></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Children</th><td class="infobox-data">6; including <a href="/wiki/Ajit_Singh_(politician)" title="Ajit Singh (politician)">Ajit Singh</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Alma_mater" title="Alma mater">Alma mater</a></th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Dr._Bhim_Rao_Ambedkar_University,_Agra" class="mw-redirect" title="Dr. Bhim Rao Ambedkar University, Agra">Agra University</a></td></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-below" style="border-top: 1px solid right; font-size: 95%"><div></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Chaudhary Charan Singh</b> (23 December 1902 – 29 May 1987) served as the <a href="/wiki/List_of_Prime_Ministers_of_India" class="mw-redirect" title="List of Prime Ministers of India">5th</a> <a href="/wiki/Prime_minister_of_India" class="mw-redirect" title="Prime minister of India">prime minister of India</a> between 28 July 1979 and 14 January 1980. Historians and people alike frequently refer to him as the 'champion of India's peasants'.<sup id="cite_ref-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life">Early life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Charan_Singh&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Early life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Charan Singh was born on 23 December 1902 in a rural peasant family of the <a href="/wiki/Tewatia" title="Tewatia">Tewatia Jat</a> clan<sup id="cite_ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> of village <a href="/wiki/Noorpur,_Uttar_Pradesh" title="Noorpur, Uttar Pradesh">Noorpur</a>, <a href="/wiki/United_Provinces_of_Agra_and_Oudh" title="United Provinces of Agra and Oudh">United Provinces of Agra and Oudh</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Charan Singh entered politics as part of the <a href="/wiki/Indian_Independence_Movement" class="mw-redirect" title="Indian Independence Movement">Indian Independence Movement</a> motivated by <a href="/wiki/Mahatma_Gandhi" title="Mahatma Gandhi">Mahatma Gandhi</a>. He was active from 1931 in the Ghaziabad District <a href="/wiki/Arya_Samaj" title="Arya Samaj">Arya Samaj</a> as well as the Meerut District <a href="/wiki/Indian_National_Congress" title="Indian National Congress">Indian National Congress</a> for which he was jailed twice by the British. Before independence, as a member of Legislative Assembly of the United Provinces elected in 1937, he took a deep interest in the laws that were detrimental to the village economy and he slowly built his ideological and practical stand against the exploitation of tillers of the land by landlords.
</p><p>Between 1952 and 1967, he was one of "three principal leaders in Congress state politics." He became particularly notable in <a href="/wiki/Uttar_Pradesh" title="Uttar Pradesh">Uttar Pradesh</a> from the 1950s for drafting and ensuring the passage of what were then the most revolutionary land reform laws in any state in India under the tutelage of the then Chief Minister <a href="/wiki/Govind_Ballabh_Pant" title="Govind Ballabh Pant">Govind Ballabh Pant</a>; first as <a href="/wiki/Parliamentary_Secretary" class="mw-redirect" title="Parliamentary Secretary">Parliamentary Secretary</a> and then as Revenue Minister responsible for Land Reforms. He became visible on the national stage from 1959 when he publicly opposed the unquestioned leader and Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Jawaharlal_Nehru" title="Jawaharlal Nehru">Jawaharlal Nehru's</a> socialistic and collectivist land policies in the Nagpur Congress Session. Though his position in the faction-ridden Uttar Pradesh Congress was weakened, this was a point when the middle peasant communities across castes in North India began looking up to him as their spokesperson and later as their unquestioned leader. Singh stood for tight government spending, enforced consequences for corrupt officers, and advocated a "firm hand in dealing with the demands of government employees for increased wages and dearness allowances." It is also worth noting that within the factional Uttar Pradesh Congress, his ability to articulate his clear policies and values made him stand out from his colleagues. Following this period, Charan Singh defected from the Congress on 1 April 1967, joined the opposition party, and became the first non-Congress chief minister of UP.<sup id="cite_ref-brass_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-brass-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> This was a period when non-Congress governments were a strong force in India from 1967 to 1971.
</p><p>As leader of the <a href="/wiki/Bharatiya_Lok_Dal" title="Bharatiya Lok Dal">Bharatiya Lok Dal</a>, a major constituent of the <a href="/wiki/Janata_Party" title="Janata Party">Janata coalition</a>, he was disappointed in his ambition to become Prime Minister in 1977 by <a href="/wiki/Jayaprakash_Narayan" title="Jayaprakash Narayan">Jayaprakash Narayan's</a> choice of <a href="/wiki/Morarji_Desai" title="Morarji Desai">Morarji Desai</a>.
</p><p>During <a href="/wiki/1977_Indian_general_election" title="1977 Indian general election">1977 Lok Sabha Elections</a>, the fragmented opposition united a few months before the elections under the Janata Party banner, for which Chaudhary Charan Singh had been struggling almost single-handedly since 1974. It was because of the efforts of <a href="/wiki/Raj_Narain" title="Raj Narain">Raj Narain</a> that he became Prime Minister in the year 1979 though Raj Narain was Chairman of Janata Party-Secular and assured Charan Singh of elevating him as Prime Minister, the way he helped him to become Chief Minister in the year 1967 in Uttar Pradesh. However, he resigned after just 24 weeks in office when <a href="/wiki/Indira_Gandhi" title="Indira Gandhi">Indira Gandhi's</a> <a href="/wiki/Indian_National_Congress" title="Indian National Congress">Congress Party</a> withdrew support to the government. Charan Singh said he resigned because he was not ready to be blackmailed into withdrawing Indira Gandhi's emergency-related court cases.<sup id="cite_ref-Charan_singh_resigns_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Charan_singh_resigns-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> Fresh elections were held six months later. Charan Singh continued to lead the Lok Dal in opposition until his death in 1987.
</p>
<h2><span id="Early_years_.E2.80.93_pre-Independence_India"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_years_–_pre-Independence_India">Early years – pre-Independence India</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Charan_Singh&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Early years – pre-Independence India">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1097763485"><table class="box-Unreferenced_section plainlinks metadata ambox ambox-content ambox-Unreferenced" role="presentation"><tbody><tr><td class="mbox-image"><div class="mbox-image-div"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Question_book-new.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/99/Question_book-new.svg/50px-Question_book-new.svg.png" decoding="async" width="50" height="39" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/99/Question_book-new.svg/75px-Question_book-new.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/99/Question_book-new.svg/100px-Question_book-new.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="512" data-file-height="399" /></a></span></div></td><td class="mbox-text"><div class="mbox-text-span">This section <b>does not <a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources" title="Wikipedia:Citing sources">cite</a> any <a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability" title="Wikipedia:Verifiability">sources</a></b>.<span class="hide-when-compact"> Please help <a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Charan_Singh&amp;action=edit">improve this section</a> by <a href="/wiki/Help:Referencing_for_beginners" title="Help:Referencing for beginners">adding citations to reliable sources</a>. Unsourced material may be challenged and <a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability#Burden_of_evidence" title="Wikipedia:Verifiability">removed</a>.</span>  <span class="date-container"><i>(<span class="date">October 2012</span>)</i></span><span class="hide-when-compact"><i> (<small><a href="/wiki/Help:Maintenance_template_removal" title="Help:Maintenance template removal">Learn how and when to remove this template message</a></small>)</i></span></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>Charan Singh's ancestor was a prominent leader of the <a href="/wiki/Indian_Rebellion_of_1857" title="Indian Rebellion of 1857">Indian Rebellion of 1857</a>, <a href="/wiki/Raja_Nahar_Singh" class="mw-redirect" title="Raja Nahar Singh">Raja Nahar Singh</a> of <a href="/wiki/Ballabhgarh" title="Ballabhgarh">Ballabhgarh</a> (in present-day Haryana). Nahar Singh was sent to the gallows in <a href="/wiki/Chandni_Chowk" title="Chandni Chowk">Chandni Chowk</a>, <a href="/wiki/Delhi" title="Delhi">Delhi</a>. In order to escape the oppression from the <a href="/wiki/British_Raj" title="British Raj">British Government</a> following their defeat, the Maharaja's followers, including Charan Singh's grandfather moved eastward to district Bulandshaher in Uttar Pradesh.
</p><p>Charan Singh was born on 23 December 1902 in the village of <a href="/wiki/Noorpur,_Uttar_Pradesh" title="Noorpur, Uttar Pradesh">Noorpur</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Meerut_district" title="Meerut district">Meerut district</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Rohilkhand" title="Rohilkhand">Rohilkhand</a> region of the <a href="/wiki/Western_Uttar_Pradesh" title="Western Uttar Pradesh">North-Western</a> part of the <a href="/wiki/United_Provinces_of_Agra_and_Oudh" title="United Provinces of Agra and Oudh">United Provinces of Agra and Oudh</a> in <a href="/wiki/British_Raj" title="British Raj">British India</a> (present-day in <a href="/wiki/Bijnor_district" title="Bijnor district">Bijnor district</a>, <a href="/wiki/Uttar_Pradesh" title="Uttar Pradesh">Uttar Pradesh</a>, <a href="/wiki/India" title="India">India</a>) into a rural peasant <a href="/wiki/Jats#Hindu_Jats" title="Jats">Hindu Jat</a> family of the <a href="/wiki/Teotia" title="Teotia">Teotia</a> clan.<sup id="cite_ref-:1_9-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup>  He was a good student, and received a Master of Arts (MA) degree in 1925 and a law degree in 1926 from Agra University. He started practice as a civil lawyer at <a href="/wiki/Ghaziabad,_Uttar_Pradesh" class="mw-redirect" title="Ghaziabad, Uttar Pradesh">Ghaziabad</a> in 1928.
</p><p>In February 1937 he was elected from the constituency of <a href="/wiki/Chhaprauli" title="Chhaprauli">Chhaprauli</a> (Baghpat) to the Legislative Assembly of the United Provinces at the age of 34.<sup id="cite_ref-:1_9-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> In 1938 he introduced an Agricultural Produce Market Bill in the Assembly which was published in the issues of The <a href="/wiki/Hindustan_Times" title="Hindustan Times">Hindustan Times</a> of Delhi dated 31 March 1938. The Bill was intended to safeguard the interests of the farmers against the rapacity of traders. The Bill was adopted by most of the States in India, <a href="/wiki/Punjab_(India)" class="mw-redirect" title="Punjab (India)">Punjab</a> being the first state to do so in 1940.
</p><p>Charan Singh followed <a href="/wiki/Mahatma_Gandhi" title="Mahatma Gandhi">Mahatma Gandhi</a> in non-violent struggle for independence from the British Government, and was imprisoned several times. In 1930, he was sent to jail for 12 years by the British for contravention of the salt laws. He was jailed again for one year in November 1940 for individual Satyagraha movement. In August 1942 he was jailed again by the British under DIR and released in November 1943.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Independent_India">Independent India</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Charan_Singh&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Independent India">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Charan Singh opposed <a href="/wiki/Jawaharlal_Nehru" title="Jawaharlal Nehru">Jawaharlal Nehru</a> on his Soviet-style economic reforms. Charan Singh was of the opinion that cooperative farms would not succeed in India. Being a son of a farmer, Charan Singh opined that the right of ownership was important to the farmer in remaining a cultivator. He wanted to preserve and stabilize a system of peasant proprietorship.<sup id="cite_ref-brass_7-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-brass-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> Charan Singh's political career suffered due to his open criticism of Nehru's economic policy.
</p><p>Charan Singh left the Congress party in 1967, and formed his own political party, <a href="/wiki/Bharatiya_Kranti_Dal" title="Bharatiya Kranti Dal">Bharatiya Kranti Dal</a>. With the help and support of <a href="/wiki/Raj_Narain" title="Raj Narain">Raj Narain</a> and <a href="/wiki/Ram_Manohar_Lohia" title="Ram Manohar Lohia">Ram Manohar Lohia</a>, he became <a href="/wiki/Chief_Minister_of_Uttar_Pradesh" class="mw-redirect" title="Chief Minister of Uttar Pradesh">Chief Minister of Uttar Pradesh</a> in 1967, and later in 1970. In 1975, he was jailed again, but this time by then Indian Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Indira_Gandhi" title="Indira Gandhi">Indira Gandhi</a>, daughter of his former rival Nehru. She had declared the <a href="/wiki/Indian_Emergency_(1975-1977)" class="mw-redirect" title="Indian Emergency (1975-1977)">state of emergency</a> and jailed all her political opponents. In the <a href="/wiki/1977_Indian_general_election" title="1977 Indian general election">1977 general elections</a>, the Indian populace voted her out, and the opposition party, of which Chaudhary Charan Singh was a senior leader came into power. He served as <a href="/wiki/Deputy_Prime_Minister_of_India" title="Deputy Prime Minister of India">Deputy Prime Minister</a>, <a href="/wiki/Home_Minister_of_India" class="mw-redirect" title="Home Minister of India">Home Minister</a> and Finance minister in the <a href="/wiki/Premiership_of_Morarji_Desai" title="Premiership of Morarji Desai">Janata government headed by Morarji Desai</a>.
</p>
<h3><span id="First_term_as_Chief_Minister_of_Uttar_Pradesh_.281967_.E2.80.93_1968.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="First_term_as_Chief_Minister_of_Uttar_Pradesh_(1967_–_1968)">First term as Chief Minister of Uttar Pradesh (1967 – 1968)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Charan_Singh&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: First term as Chief Minister of Uttar Pradesh (1967 – 1968)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Charan Singh for the first time, became <a href="/wiki/Chief_Minister_of_Uttar_Pradesh" class="mw-redirect" title="Chief Minister of Uttar Pradesh">Chief Minister of Uttar Pradesh</a> on 3 April 1967 with the help of <a href="/wiki/Samyukta_Vidhayak_Dal" title="Samyukta Vidhayak Dal">Samyukta Vidhayak Dal</a> coalition.<sup id="cite_ref-:1_9-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Samyukta_Vidhayak_Dal" title="Samyukta Vidhayak Dal">Samyukta Vidhayak Dal</a> was formed after failure of negotiations between Charan Singh and <a href="/wiki/Chandra_Bhanu_Gupta" title="Chandra Bhanu Gupta">Chandra Bhanu Gupta</a> on the composition of Gupta's ministry. Singh wanted some of his allies like <a href="/wiki/Jai_Ram_Varma" title="Jai Ram Varma">Jai Ram Varma</a> and Udit Narain Sharma to be included in the cabinet and removal of some of the men from the cabinet. As a result of failure of negotiations, Charan Singh with his 16 MLAs defected from <a href="/wiki/Indian_National_Congress" title="Indian National Congress">Congress</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Samyukta_Vidhayak_Dal" title="Samyukta Vidhayak Dal">Samyukta Vidhayak Dal</a> was coalition formed with the help of non-Congress parties like <a href="/wiki/Bharatiya_Jana_Sangh" title="Bharatiya Jana Sangh">Bharatiya Jana Sangh</a>, <a href="/wiki/Samyukta_Socialist_Party" title="Samyukta Socialist Party">Samyukta Socialist Party</a>, <a href="/wiki/Communist_Party_of_India" title="Communist Party of India">Communist Party of India</a>, <a href="/wiki/Swatantra_Party" title="Swatantra Party">Swatantra Party</a>, <a href="/wiki/Praja_Socialist_Party" title="Praja Socialist Party">Praja Socialist Party</a>, <a href="/wiki/Republican_Party_of_India" title="Republican Party of India">Republican Party of India</a>, <a href="/wiki/Communist_Party_of_India_(Marxist)" title="Communist Party of India (Marxist)">Communist Party of India (Marxist)</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> Within months of his government formation disputes started to arise in SVD coalition. <a href="/wiki/Samyukta_Socialist_Party" title="Samyukta Socialist Party">Samyukta Socialist Party</a>, one of the constituent of this coalition, demanded to completely abolish the land revenue or at least abolish on uneconomic lands but Charan Singh refused to accept this demand as he was worried about the revenue generation and resources.<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Praja_Socialist_Party" title="Praja Socialist Party">Praja Socialist Party</a>, another constituent in this coalition, demanded for the release of government employees held in preventive detention for their strikes but this demand also Singh refused to accept.<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The disputes between Charan Singh and <a href="/wiki/Samyukta_Socialist_Party" title="Samyukta Socialist Party">Samyukta Socialist Party</a> became public when SSP decided to launch an agitation of <i>Angrezi Hatao</i> (get rid to English) and during this movement two of its ministers courted arrest.<sup id="cite_ref-:1_9-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-:4_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> SSP withdrew from coalition on 5 January 1968.<sup id="cite_ref-:4_14-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> On 17 February 1968, Charan Singh submitted his resignation to the governor <a href="/wiki/Bezawada_Gopala_Reddy" title="Bezawada Gopala Reddy">Bezawada Gopala Reddy</a> and on 25 February 1968, President's rule was imposed on <a href="/wiki/Uttar_Pradesh" title="Uttar Pradesh">Uttar Pradesh</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Second_term_as_Chief_Minister_of_Uttar_Pradesh_.281970.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Second_term_as_Chief_Minister_of_Uttar_Pradesh_(1970)">Second term as Chief Minister of Uttar Pradesh (1970)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Charan_Singh&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Second term as Chief Minister of Uttar Pradesh (1970)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>After the split in <a href="/wiki/Indian_National_Congress" title="Indian National Congress">Congress</a> party, <a href="/wiki/Chandra_Bhanu_Gupta" title="Chandra Bhanu Gupta">Chandra Bhanu Gupta</a> resigned as Chief Minister on 10 February 1970.<sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> On 18 February 1970, Charan Singh became <a href="/wiki/Chief_Minister_of_Uttar_Pradesh" class="mw-redirect" title="Chief Minister of Uttar Pradesh">Chief Minister of Uttar Pradesh</a> for the second time with the help of <a href="/wiki/Indira_Gandhi" title="Indira Gandhi">Indira Gandhi</a>'s <a href="/wiki/Congress_(R)" class="mw-redirect" title="Congress (R)">Congress (R)</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-:5_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:5-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> After three <a href="/wiki/Rajya_Sabha" title="Rajya Sabha">Rajya Sabha</a> members of <a href="/wiki/Bharatiya_Kranti_Dal" title="Bharatiya Kranti Dal">Bharatiya Kranti Dal</a> voted against the decision of <a href="/wiki/Indira_Gandhi" title="Indira Gandhi">Indira Gandhi</a> to eliminate the <a href="/wiki/Privy_Purse_in_India" title="Privy Purse in India">Privy Purse</a>, <a href="/wiki/Kamalapati_Tripathi" title="Kamalapati Tripathi">Kamalapati Tripathi</a> announced the withdrawal of the support of <a href="/wiki/Indian_National_Congress_(R)" title="Indian National Congress (R)">Congress (R)</a> for the Singh's government.<sup id="cite_ref-:6_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:6-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> Charan Singh demanded the resignation of 14 <a href="/wiki/Indian_National_Congress_(R)" title="Indian National Congress (R)">Congress (R)</a> minister but they refused to resign.<sup id="cite_ref-:6_20-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:6-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> On 27 September 1970, governor <a href="/wiki/Bezawada_Gopala_Reddy" title="Bezawada Gopala Reddy">Bezawada Gopala Reddy</a> accepted the resignation of ministers but also asked Charan Singh to resign.<sup id="cite_ref-:5_19-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:5-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 1 October 1970, <a href="/wiki/President%27s_rule" title="President&#39;s rule">President's rule</a> was imposed on <a href="/wiki/Uttar_Pradesh" title="Uttar Pradesh">Uttar Pradesh</a> by <a href="/wiki/V._V._Giri" title="V. V. Giri">V. V. Giri</a> from <a href="/wiki/Kiev" class="mw-redirect" title="Kiev">Kiev</a>, who was on tour there.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> Just two weeks later with the recalling of the Uttar Pradesh assembly, <a href="/wiki/Tribhuvan_Narain_Singh" title="Tribhuvan Narain Singh">Tribhuvan Narain Singh</a> was elected the leader of the house and became <a href="/wiki/Chief_Minister_of_Uttar_Pradesh" class="mw-redirect" title="Chief Minister of Uttar Pradesh">Chief Minister of Uttar Pradesh</a> with the support of <a href="/wiki/Congress_(O)" class="mw-redirect" title="Congress (O)">Congress (O)</a>, <a href="/wiki/Bharatiya_Jana_Sangh" title="Bharatiya Jana Sangh">Bharatiya Jana Sangh</a>, <a href="/wiki/Swatantra_Party" title="Swatantra Party">Swatantra Party</a> and <a href="/wiki/Samyukta_Socialist_Party" title="Samyukta Socialist Party">Samyukta Socialist Party</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Minister_of_Home_Affairs_.281977_.E2.80.93_1978.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Minister_of_Home_Affairs_(1977_–_1978)">Minister of Home Affairs (1977 – 1978)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Charan_Singh&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Minister of Home Affairs (1977 – 1978)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Charan Singh became Cabinet minister in <a href="/wiki/Morarji_Desai_ministry" class="mw-redirect" title="Morarji Desai ministry">Morarji Desai government</a> and took the office as <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Home_Affairs_(India)" title="Minister of Home Affairs (India)">Minister of Home Affairs</a> on 24 March 1977.<sup id="cite_ref-:1_9-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> As a Home Minister, Charan Singh took the decision to dissolve all the state assemblies which were under <a href="/wiki/Congress_(India)" class="mw-redirect" title="Congress (India)">Congress</a> rule. He argued that these assemblies no longer represent the will of the electorate of their respective states.<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> Charan Singh wrote the letter to nine Chief Ministers to advise their governors to dissolve their state assemblies.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>  Chief Minister of these states went to <a href="/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_India" title="Supreme Court of India">Supreme Court</a> against this dissolution but the dismissals were validated by Supreme Court.<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 3 October 1977, Charan Singh got <a href="/wiki/Indira_Gandhi" title="Indira Gandhi">Indira Gandhi</a> arrested from her 12 Willingdon Crescent residence.<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> The charges against her were that during <a href="/wiki/1977_Indian_general_election" title="1977 Indian general election">1977 election</a>, she misused her position to get jeeps for election campaigns and another charge was related to contract between the <a href="/wiki/ONGC" class="mw-redirect" title="ONGC">ONGC</a> and the French oil company CFP.<sup id="cite_ref-:7_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:7-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> But the magistrate before whom she appeared, released her stating that there was no evidence to back up the arrest.<sup id="cite_ref-:7_33-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:7-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> By botching up the arrest, Singh prepared his resignation letter but <a href="/wiki/Morarji_Desai" title="Morarji Desai">Morarji Desai</a> did not accept it.<sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 1 July 1978, Charan Singh resigned from the cabinet of <a href="/wiki/Morarji_Desai_ministry" class="mw-redirect" title="Morarji Desai ministry">Morarji Desai</a> because of growing differences between them over trial of <a href="/wiki/Indira_Gandhi" title="Indira Gandhi">Indira Gandhi</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-:1_9-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> In December 1978, Singh wanted to undo <a href="/wiki/Janata_Party" title="Janata Party">Janata Party</a> and wanted coalition government in place of Janata Party government.<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> On 24 January 1979, Singh returned into cabinet and held two portfolios of <a href="/wiki/Deputy_Prime_Minister_of_India" title="Deputy Prime Minister of India">Deputy Prime Minister</a> and <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Finance_(India)" title="Minister of Finance (India)">Minister of Finance</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-:1_9-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Prime_Ministership">Prime Ministership</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Charan_Singh&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Prime Ministership">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>When the Janata Party won the <a href="/wiki/1977_Indian_general_election" title="1977 Indian general election">Lok Sabha elections in 1977</a>, its MPs authorized the elder guiding lights of the movement, <a href="/wiki/Jayaprakash_Narayan" title="Jayaprakash Narayan">Jayaprakash Narayan</a> and <a href="/wiki/Acharya_Kripalani" class="mw-redirect" title="Acharya Kripalani">Acharya Kripalani</a> to choose candidate for the post of Prime Minister. Morarji Desai was chosen and he named Singh Home Minister. Singh was asked to resign in June 1978 following disagreements with Desai, but was brought back to the cabinet as Deputy Prime Minister in January 1979. In 1979, the Janata government began to unravel over the issue of the dual loyalties of some members to Janata and the <a href="/wiki/Rashtriya_Swayamsevak_Sangh" title="Rashtriya Swayamsevak Sangh">Rashtriya Swayamsevak Sangh</a> (RSS)—the <a href="/wiki/Hindu_nationalism" title="Hindu nationalism">Hindu nationalist</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-Horowitz_39-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Horowitz-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Haynes2003_40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Haynes2003-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Paramilitary" title="Paramilitary">paramilitary</a><sup id="cite_ref-McLeod2002_41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-McLeod2002-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> organisation. Singh, who as the Union home minister during the previous year had ordered the Gandhi's' arrests, took advantage of this and started courting Indira Gandhi's Congress (I) party. After a significant exodus from the Janata party to Singh's faction, Morarji Desai resigned as prime minister in July 1979. Singh was appointed prime minister, by President Reddy, after Gandhi and <a href="/wiki/Sanjay_Gandhi" title="Sanjay Gandhi">Sanjay Gandhi</a> promised Singh that Congress (I) would support his government from outside on certain conditions. Singh was sworn in as Prime Minister on 28 July 1979, with outside support from Indira Gandhi's Congress (I) party and with <a href="/wiki/Yeshwantrao_Chavan" class="mw-redirect" title="Yeshwantrao Chavan">Yeshwantrao Chavan</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Indian_National_Congress_(Socialist)" class="mw-redirect" title="Indian National Congress (Socialist)">Congress (Socialist)</a> party as his Deputy Prime Minister.<sup id="cite_ref-Supporting_Charan_Singh_42-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Supporting_Charan_Singh-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Charan_Singh_43-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Charan_Singh-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> Indira's conditions included dropping all charges against her and Sanjay. Since Singh refused to drop them, Congress withdrew its support just before Singh was to confirm his majority in the Lok Sabha. He resigned as prime minister on 20 August 1979, after just 23 days in office, becoming the only Prime Minister never to obtain the confidence of Parliament. Singh then advised President <a href="/wiki/Neelam_Sanjiva_Reddy" title="Neelam Sanjiva Reddy">Neelam Sanjiva Reddy</a> to dissolve the Lok Sabha. Janata Party leader <a href="/wiki/Jagjivan_Ram" title="Jagjivan Ram">Jagjivan Ram</a> challenged that advice and sought time to cobble support, but the Lok Sabha was dissolved, and Charan Singh continued as caretaker Prime Minister until January 1980.<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Later_years">Later years</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Charan_Singh&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Later years">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>On 26 September 1979, he formed <a href="/wiki/Lokdal" title="Lokdal">Lok Dal</a> by merging <a href="/wiki/Janata_Party_(Secular)" title="Janata Party (Secular)">Janata Party (Secular)</a>, Socialist Party and Orissa Janata Party.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_45-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> He was elected president of <a href="/wiki/Lokdal" title="Lokdal">Lok Dal</a> and <a href="/wiki/Raj_Narain" title="Raj Narain">Raj Narain</a> was elected as its working president.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_45-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> In August 1982, a major split occurred in <a href="/wiki/Lokdal" title="Lokdal">Lok Dal</a>, with one faction of Charan Singh and another consisted of <a href="/wiki/Karpoori_Thakur" title="Karpoori Thakur">Karpoori Thakur</a>, <a href="/wiki/Madhu_Limaye" title="Madhu Limaye">Madhu Limaye</a>, <a href="/wiki/Biju_Patnaik" title="Biju Patnaik">Biju Patnaik</a>, <a href="/wiki/Devi_Lal" title="Devi Lal">Devi Lal</a>, <a href="/wiki/George_Fernandes" title="George Fernandes">George Fernandes</a> and <a href="/wiki/Kumbha_Ram_Arya" title="Kumbha Ram Arya">Kumbha Ram Arya</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 21 October 1984, Charan Singh founded a new party <a href="/wiki/Dalit_Mazdoor_Kisan_Party" title="Dalit Mazdoor Kisan Party">Dalit Mazdoor Kisan Party</a>, by merging <a href="/wiki/Lokdal" title="Lokdal">Lok Dal</a>, Democratic Socialist Party of <a href="/wiki/Hemwati_Nandan_Bahuguna" title="Hemwati Nandan Bahuguna">Hemwati Nandan Bahuguna</a>, <a href="/wiki/Rashtriya_Congress" title="Rashtriya Congress">Rashtriya Congress</a> of <a href="/wiki/Ratubhai_Adani" title="Ratubhai Adani">Ratubhai Adani</a> and some leaders of <a href="/wiki/Janata_Party" title="Janata Party">Janata Party</a> like <a href="/wiki/Devi_Lal" title="Devi Lal">Devi Lal</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> Later it changed its name back to the Lok Dal.<sup id="cite_ref-:2_50-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:2-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Personal_life">Personal life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Charan_Singh&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Personal life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Charan Singh had six children with wife <a href="/wiki/Gayatri_Devi_(Uttar_Pradesh_politician)" title="Gayatri Devi (Uttar Pradesh politician)">Gayatri Devi</a> (1905-2002). <a href="/wiki/Gayatri_Devi_(Uttar_Pradesh_politician)" title="Gayatri Devi (Uttar Pradesh politician)">Gayatri Devi</a> was elected an MLA from <a href="/wiki/Iglas_(Assembly_constituency)" class="mw-redirect" title="Iglas (Assembly constituency)">Iglas</a> in 1969, from Gokul in 1974, then elected to Lok Sabha from <a href="/wiki/Kairana_(Lok_Sabha_constituency)" class="mw-redirect" title="Kairana (Lok Sabha constituency)">Kairana</a> in 1980, and lost Lok Sabha election from <a href="/wiki/Mathura_(Lok_Sabha_constituency)" class="mw-redirect" title="Mathura (Lok Sabha constituency)">Mathura</a> in 1984. His son <a href="/wiki/Ajit_Singh_(Indian_politician)" class="mw-redirect" title="Ajit Singh (Indian politician)">Ajit Singh</a> was the president of a political party <a href="/wiki/Rashtriya_Lok_Dal" title="Rashtriya Lok Dal">Rashtriya Lok Dal</a> and a former Union Minister and a many times Member of Parliament. Ajit Singh's son <a href="/wiki/Jayant_Chaudhary" class="mw-redirect" title="Jayant Chaudhary">Jayant Chaudhary</a> was elected to <a href="/wiki/15th_Lok_Sabha" title="15th Lok Sabha">15th Lok Sabha</a> from Mathura, which he lost to <a href="/wiki/Hema_Malini" title="Hema Malini">Hema Malini</a> in the election of 2014.
</p><p>Singh suffered a <a href="/wiki/Stroke" title="Stroke">stroke</a> on 29 November 1985. He could not completely recover from the condition despite being treated the following March at a hospital in the US. At 11:35&#160;p.m. (<a href="/wiki/Indian_Standard_Time" title="Indian Standard Time">IST</a>) on 28 May 1987 doctors were called for to his residence in <a href="/wiki/New_Delhi" title="New Delhi">New Delhi</a>, after his respiration was found "unsteady". Efforts to revive him failed and was declared dead at 2:35&#160;a.m. (IST) the following morning after a "cardiovascular collapse".<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy">Legacy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Charan_Singh&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Legacy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Category:Memorials_to_Chaudhary_Charan_Singh" title="Category:Memorials to Chaudhary Charan Singh">Category:Memorials to Chaudhary Charan Singh</a></div>
<p>He was the chief architect of land reforms in U.P.; he took a leading part in formulation and finalization of the Dept. Redemption Bill 1939, which brought great relief to rural debtors. It was also at his initiative that the salaries and other privileges enjoyed by Ministers in U.P. were drastically reduced. As Chief Minister he was instrumental in bringing about the Land Holding Act 1960 which was aimed at lowering the ceiling on land holdings to make it uniform throughout the State.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (January 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
Since his death, many who knew Singh have ensured his life and work are remembered positively. These perceptions enforce the notion that he was of a "higher category of leaders" in the areas of "intellect, personal integrity, and . . . coherence of his economic and social thought."<sup id="cite_ref-brass_7-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-brass-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> His association with causes dear to farming communities in India caused his memorial in New Delhi to be named <i><a href="/wiki/Raj_Ghat_and_other_memorials" class="mw-redirect" title="Raj Ghat and other memorials">Kisan Ghat</a></i> (in <a href="/wiki/Hindi" title="Hindi">Hindi</a>, Kisan is the word for farmer and Ghat is word for river bank). His birthday on 23 December is celebrated as <a href="/w/index.php?title=Kisan_Diwas&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Kisan Diwas (page does not exist)">Kisan Diwas</a> in India. A commemorative <a href="/wiki/Postage_stamp" title="Postage stamp">postage stamp</a> was issued by the government of India on the third death anniversary (29 May 1990) of Charan Singh.
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Amausi_Airport" class="mw-redirect" title="Amausi Airport">Amausi Airport</a> in <a href="/wiki/Lucknow" title="Lucknow">Lucknow</a>, <a href="/wiki/Uttar_Pradesh" title="Uttar Pradesh">Uttar Pradesh</a> was renamed Chaudhary Charan Singh International Airport after him, and the University of <a href="/wiki/Meerut" title="Meerut">Meerut</a> in Uttar Pradesh, India, was also named <a href="/wiki/Chaudhary_Charan_Singh_University" title="Chaudhary Charan Singh University">Chaudhary Charan Singh University</a> in his honour.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="In_popular_culture">In popular culture</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Charan_Singh&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: In popular culture">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p><i>Kisan Kranti Ke Praneta - Ch. Charan Singh</i> is a 1996 short documentary film directed by Ashok Vazirani and produced by the <a href="/wiki/Films_Division_of_India" title="Films Division of India">Films Division of India</a> which covers the life and achievements of the prime minister including his contributions to the <a href="/wiki/Agriculture_in_India" title="Agriculture in India">Indian agriculture sector</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-:3_52-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:3-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> Charan Singh has also been portrayed by Anwar Fatehan in the 2013 television series <i><a href="/wiki/Pradhanmantri" title="Pradhanmantri">Pradhanmantri</a></i> (<abbr style="font-size:85%" title="literal translation">lit.</abbr><span style="white-space: nowrap;">&#8201;</span>'Prime Minister&#39;), which covers the tenures of Indian PMs,<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> by Sundaram in the 2019 film <i><a href="/wiki/NTR:_Mahanayakudu" title="NTR: Mahanayakudu">NTR: Mahanayakudu</a></i> which is based on the life of Indian actor-politician <a href="/wiki/N._T._Rama_Rao" title="N. T. Rama Rao">N. T. Rama Rao</a>.,<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> and by <a href="/wiki/Govind_Namdeo" class="mw-redirect" title="Govind Namdeo">Govind Namdeo</a> in the 2021 film <i><a href="/wiki/Main_Mulayam_Singh_Yadav" title="Main Mulayam Singh Yadav">Main Mulayam Singh Yadav</a></i> which charts the life of former Uttar Pradesh CM <a href="/wiki/Mulayam_Singh_Yadav" title="Mulayam Singh Yadav">Mulayam Singh Yadav</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Books">Books</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Charan_Singh&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Books">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<ul><li><i>Agrarian Revolution in Uttar Pradesh</i> (1957)<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><i>Joint Farming X-rayed</i> (1959)<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><i>India's Economic Policy – The Gandhian Blueprint</i> (1978)<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><i>Economic Nightmare of India: Its Cause and Cure</i> (1981)<sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><i>Abolition of Zamindari (1947)</i><sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><i>Whither Co-operative Farming (1956)</i><sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><i>Prevention of Division of Holdings Below a Certain Minimum</i></li>
<li><i>India’s Poverty and It’s Solution</i> (1964)<sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><i>Land Reforms in UP and the Kulaks (1986)</i><sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
